Second Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1059. CHARLES LEDGERWOOD and WILLIAM WHEELER were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of February, 3 cast-iron ornaments, value 9s., the goods of Henry Dartnell.
JAMES CHETTLE GOMME. I had the care of a house at Hammersmith, which belonged to Mr. Dartnell; a great many ornaments had been taken from the top of the iron palisades, in front of the house - I missed some on the 20th of February; there was a man in the house to take care of it, who gave me information.
RICHARD READING. I have the care of that house; it belongs to Mr. Henry Dartnell - Mr. Gomme had the care of it under him; on the 18th of February I got up to go to work at six o'clock in the morning - I missed some knobs; I came home at six in the evening, and watched from six till twelve - I then went to bed, and in the morning I missed fourteen more; I told Mr. Gomme of it, and the constable found some of them at a shop at Hammersmith - I gave him one to find the other; I have seen then fitted, and they match exactly - I have no doubt they were taken from there; I know the prisoners do not live far off.
MARY MARTIN. I keep an iron-shop at Hammersmith. On the 20th of February, the prisoners came together to my shop, and brought three knobs of iron; I asked how they came by them - they said, they came from near the new bridge, that the barges were fastened to them, and had loosened them; I cannot tell whether they said they got them out of the Thames, or out of the sewers - I put them among my iron, and gave them to the officer; I gave a farthing per lb. for them.
MARY ANN MARTIN. I live with my mother; the prisoners came to my mother about one o'clock on the 20th of February, with three knobs, which they said they got out of the sewer.
EDWARD EDGSON. I am a constable; I took these knobs from Martin's - I showed then to the witness; I took the prisoner, who said, at first, they had not taken any; but afterwards they said, they had taken them out of the Thames all over clay.
LEDGERWOOD's Defence. We never said any such thing; but because we were taken up, that woman and her daughter came and swore this against us; I know nothing about it.
LEDGERWOOD - GUILTY. Aged 17.
WHEELER - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18280410-269]
1060. GEORGE DAVIS and HENRY HIGGS were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 5s., the goods of John White, from his person.
DAVIS pleaded GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
JOHN WHITE. I am a chemist and druggist, and live in North-place, Gray's Inn-lane. I was standing in the Tiltyard, Westminster, last Sunday, about eleven o'clock, as they were relieving guard, intending to pass when there was an opening; I felt something at my pocket, and my handkerchief gradually left my pocket; I turned round, and Thompson had hold of Davis - Higgs was close by him.
THOMAS THOMPSON. I saw the prosecutor last Sunday morning; I saw the prisoner together try several gentlemen's pockets - they went behind the prosecutor, and Davis drew this handkerchief from his pocket; I laid hold of him, and took it from his pocket - Higgs was close to Davis, on his left side, at the time he took it; I suppose he was not three minutes in picking the pocket, but from the time he got his hand into the pocket it was not half a minute - it was deliberately done; I think Higgs must have seen what Davis did.
WILLIAM UPTON. I was with Thompson; we watched the two boys; I placed myself by their side in the gateway, and I fancy they thought they were observed; I sawthem try six or seven pockets; they then crossed over, and placed themselves behind the prosecutor; Davis drew the handkerchief from the pocket, and Thompson seized him immediately.
HIGGS - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280410-270]
1061. DAVID GARDINER was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 1 bridle, value 4s., the goods of Thomas Eddy.
WILLIAM WAKEFIELD. I am a carman to Mr. Thomas Eddy. I was coming out of his place into Blenheim-mews on the 7th of April, and saw the prisoner go by with this bridle on his arm, which I knew was Mr. Eddy's, as I had been in the habit of putting it on the horse every day - I said to a person named Kitchen, "That man has got a bridle, and we had better go and take him," which we did- I have known him some time; I believe he had an honest character.
JOHN KITCHER. I am in the employ of Mr. Thomas Eddy. At a quarter-past seven o'clock, we saw the prisoner go by with this bridle, and took him at the corner of Blenheim-street - he said he had bought it.
ANGELIOUS BETRAUN. I am an officer, and took him.
Prisoner's Defence. Necessity and poverty led me to take it. I had been to borrow 6d. of Mr. Eddy, and he was not in the way.
GUILTY. Aged 52.
Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18280410-271]
1062. WILLIAM CONDEN and JOSEPH WATERS were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 3 stocks and centre-bits, value 10s.; 2 stocks and pin-bits, value 7s.; 5 stocks and spoon-bits, value 17s.; 1 stock and counter-sink, value 4s.; 1 stock and sinking-bit, value 5s., and 2 stocks without bits, value 4s., the goods of William Adams.
WILLIAM ADAMS. I am a carpenter; my shop is in Fox-court, Gray's Inn-lane. I left work at half-past ten o'clock on the Wednesday night, and next morning I missed the articles stated; I have known Conden from a baby - he lived very near me.
SARAH ADAMS. I am the wife of William Adams. I went into the shop, and saw some planes lying in the yard; I then looked, and missed the articles stated, from the shelf.
HANNAH CRUMP. I live in Blewitt's-buildings. On the 13th of March, I went into Elizabeth Carr's room, No. 4, Pheasant-buildings, Gray's Inn-lane, about a quarter before seven o'clock, to give her a light; I saw Waters there, counting over some tools; as I went into the room, he pushed the basket under the bed, and one of these tools fell out, which I knew was Mr. Adams', as I had seen him work with it so frequently; I met Mr. Adam just before eleven o'clock, and asked him if he had lost anything; he said Yes, his stocks and bits; I said if he went there he would find them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you in the habit of going to his shop? A. Yes; I go for chips, and see him at work with these tools. Waters never kept company with my sister; he did with Mr. Carr's sister - I did not say anything to Waters about the tools; the tool I saw fall out, was the same as this centre-bit, but larger and darker. I do not know that Waters is a carpenter; I only know him by his coming to Mrs. Carr's - I was in the room about five minutes; I did not take up the tool.
HENRY WAGSTAFF. I did live in Pheasant-buildings. On the 13th of March, about nine o'clock in the morning, I met the two prisoners at the end of the Buildings, they asked me to give them something to drink; I said, I did not want anything myself, but I gave them some money - Conden took a stock and bit from under his coat, and asked if it was not a shame that he could not get more than 6d. for it; he said they had broken open a house, at Camden-town, which they thought was empty, but it was not - they were disturbed, but got fourteen of these tools, which they had been offered 6d. a-piece for.
Cross-examined. Q. He did not say at Fox-court? A. No. I have gone by the name of Hughes and White - I have been tried here, and was imprisoned three months. Hughes was my mother's name; and I took that after I got out of trouble. I never went by the name of Crutch.
ELIZABETH CARR. I lived in Pheasant-buildings, on the 13th of March. My husband is a fishmonger; Waters lived next door to me - on the 13th of March he brought a basket of tools into my room, about five o'clock, and asked me to let him leave it a short time; and about a quarter before eight o'clock, he came, and took it away - it had tools in it; he is a carpenter.
JOHN DAVIS. I am a constable. I took Conden on the 14th of March, and Mr. Platt took Waters; we went to Water's lodging, and found some skeleton keys; when I saw Conden, I tapped him on the shoulder, and said I wanted him for house-breaking; "Very well," says he,"I will go with you."
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you not likely to get into trouble yourself, for taking paper from the Record Office? A. No; I was employed to sell some waste paper - Mr. Allen spoke about it; he could not make a charge against me.
JAMES PLATT. As Davis was going along Gray's Inn-lane, with Conden, on the evening of the 14th, Waters came up to see if he was in custody; and as soon as he saw us, he ran away down Laystall-street - I followed, and took him; when I got him to the watch-house, I asked him where Conden was; he said, what the h - ll was that to him. Conden was then brought to the watch-house, and I said, was it not a shame to rob the poor man of his tools; he said, "You b - r, you may want a tool, before you die."
Cross-examined. Q. Are you an officer? A. No; I am a watch-case maker - Davis asked me to assist him; the tools have not been found.
WATER's Defence. They were my own tools; I left them there when I went to breakfast.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280410-272]
1063. RICHARD BUNN was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 1 bushel and a half of malt, value 12s., and 1 sack, value 1s., the goods of Solomon Chambers.
SOLOMON CHAMBERS. I am a corn-dealer, and live in James-street, Westminster, the watchman came to me about ten o'clock at night, on the 10th of April. I went to the watch-house, and saw a sack, which belonged to a miller who I do business with, and some malt in it - I cannotswear I had seen the sack that day; I found an aperture made through the partition - the prisoner was my servant, and had access to it.
JASPER FLETCHER. I am a watchman. I was going my round about eight minutes past ten o'clock, and I made a stand, as I always do, at one particular corner, I saw the prisoner come out at the wicket gate, with this malt on his shoulder; the gate opens into Brewers'-green, where Mr. Chamber's premises are - I went up to him, and asked what he had there; he said, "Malt." I asked where he was going, he said, "Round there;" I said, I should go with him, and he went round to No. 12; he made a stand, and a little girl came out. I felt the sack - I told him to put it down, and asked if Mr. Chambers knew of it; he said he did - I said I should take him and the malt to the watch-house, till I had authority from Mr. Chambers; he asked me if I knew him - I said, No; he asked if I knew his mother, Mrs. Bunn; I said, I did, by passing through my beat - he asked if I would allow him to send for her; I said Yes: by that little girl, which he did - the mother came, and asked what was the matter, and told me if I would come round the corner, she would explain things to me. I went round, and as soon as I got in at the door, she wished me for God Almighty's sake, to have nothing to do with it; as soon as I found how things were going on, I retreated away; the prisoner had then made his escape. I took the malt to the watch-house, and the prisoner was taken in bed, about twelve o'clock by the patrol.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Where did you leave him? A. By the wicket; I was not away above five minutes - I am sober now, and have drunk nothing but a pint of porter; I do not recollect whether I have been to more than one public-house.
Prisoner's Defence. The malt was not Mr. Chambers'. I cart malt and ask for the sweepings; I had saved it and meant to brew with it.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18280410-273]
Second London Jury - before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1064. GEORGE COLE and JAMES READ were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of February, 1 sheep, price 36s., the property of James Lee.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS and CRESWELL conducted the prosecution.
JAMES LEE. I am a drover, and live at Mile-end. On Sunday, the 24th of February, I received sixty-nine sheep from Mr. Gay, a Norfolk drover. I marked twenty of them along the back, and forty-nine on the shoulder, with red ochre, and forty-five of them had an O made with orange-coloured paint - I gave them to my brother to place them in a lair at the Globe public-house, at Mile-end, where they were to be left for Mr. Gay's man to drive to Smithfield.
JOSEPH LEE. I am a drover. On the 24th of February I assisted my brother to mark sixty-nine sheep, forty-nine of them on the shoulder, and one score on the back, with red ochre; I assisted John Denham to place them in the lair; that was on Sunday morning, about nine o'clock - I counted them by my brother's order, and put them into a place of security.
JOHN DENHAM. I am a drover, in the employ of Mr. Gay, who lives in Norfolk. I came up with these sheep, and assisted to put them into the lair - I went on Monday morning, at two o'clock, to take them; I found them in a different lair to the one I left them in: I counted them out of the gate, and there were but one hundred and twenty-seven - I should have had one hundred and twenty-eight; there were fifty-nine which I had from Mr. Barrell, marked with what is called a bounce mark on the shoulder, and forty-four or forty-five marked with an old brand-mark on each side, and three or four with an old mark, that we could not say what it was; they were put altogether on the Sunday evening; when I got them out of the lair I missed one; I was going up Mile-end-road, and met two of Conder's men coming back for another flock; they told me they had got one of mine at Smithfield; this was about half-past two o'clock, while I was driving the sheep to market; I got to Smithfield about four o'clock; I met Mr. Lee in Long-lane, and said to him, "I am one sheep short - I do not know whether it is one of yours;" Conder then told me the sheep was in Mr. French's pens in Smithfield; I parted Mr. Lee's sheep from the others, and found it was one of his that was missing; whether it was branded over, or whether it was the old brand-mark, I cannot say; I did not go to Mr. French's pens, for Mr. Lee said if the sheep was there he would let it be till it was day-light, as it was safe enough - I then went away.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Are you sure it was one hundred and twenty-eight sheep you had in the whole on the overnight? A. Yes; they were put in the lair about five o'clock; I saw there were forty-four or forty-five with the brand-mark.
JAMES CONDER. I am a drover. On the 24th of February I had a great many sheep to come to Smithfield; I put them into the Globe lair, Mile-end; I moved them from there at eight o'clock at night; Denham had a morning drove, which laid next to mine; when I got to Smithfield I found I had all my sheep and one over, which was marked on the shoulder with red ochre; I expected it belonged to Denham's drove, because his drove had been mixed with mine; I and my two men parted them; I mentioned that to Lee when I saw him.
JAMES GUIVER. I am a drover in the employ of Mr. Lee. On the 25th of February, between two and three o'clock in the morning, I saw Cole lead a sheep across Smithfield-market; it was marked with red-ochre between the shoulders - he took it into the lower alley, nearly opposite the Ram public-house, he put it into the second pen on the left-hand - after the man was gone, I went and looked at it, and saw the mark; I do not know what became of it afterwards.
Cross-examined. Q. What time was this? A. Between one and two; it was dark.
JOHN ALDRIDGE. I am ostler at the Ram, Smithfield. On the morning of the 25th of February, I saw the two prisoners in the Ram yard - they brought a sheep there, and asked where the sheep was that had come up by Davies' van, on the over night; I said, it was in the van. they said, they wanted that out, and to put this one in if they could - they put one in, and took another out; I asked what was to be done with it - Read said, they could not tell then, but would let me know; he came back andtold me, Barker was to drive it in the van to the Plough public-house, at Mile-end, and to have one shilling for it.
JOHN BARKER. I am a drover; this sheep was put into the van - I took it to the Plough, and received one shilling for it; I left it there in Lee's name.
THOMAS BLAKE. I am ostler at the Plough. On the morning of the 25th of February, Barker left a sheep in Lee's name - I cannot say how it was marked - a butcher called about two o'clock and took it away; he paid master the shilling.
Cross-examined. Q. You do not know the mark. A. No; it was a red mark.
WILLIAM COLESTOCK. I am a butcher, and got my sheep from the Plough by Cole's directions - Reed was by him; it was at Smithfield; they had told me, two or three days before, that they were going to have a few sheep from the country - I agreed to buy them all at 1l. each, whether little or large, and be they worth what they would; on Monday Cole said, "There is one at the Plough, fetch it away, and pay a shilling," which I did - I paid the prisoners fourteen shillings in part of the money, and next week four shillings more; I killed it at the Plough, and took it home in a bag; I live in Dock-street, Commercial-road; it was marked on the shoulder with ochre; I did not take particular notice of the mark; I cut it up and sold it in my shop; I threw the skin in a corner till it was tainted, and then I pulled the wool off - it was taken away three or four days afterwards, with others.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You did not notice the mark particularly? A. No; it was marked in the usual way.
JOHN RAE. I am an officer, and produce the wool which I found at Colestock's.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280410-274]
1065. GEORGE KNIGHT was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Sarah Grubb on the 15th of February, at St. Bridget alias St. Bride, and stealing therein, 3 gowns, value 30s.; 1 shawl, value 10s.; 8 tea-spoons, value 20s., and 1 table-spoon, value 10s., her property.
SARAH GRUBB. I am a widow and lodge in a house in the parish of St. Brides. On the 15th of February, at ten o'clock in the morning I went out - I left my room door locked; I returned at six o'clock in the evening and found my little boy there. I missed eight silver teaspoons, one silver table-spoon, three gowns and one shawl; they had been taken from my drawers - I had seen my black gown in the drawer the night before, and the spoons the day before; I had seen the other two gowns in the course of that week, and the shawl I had worn; I have seen them all since, they are in Court - I have looked well at them, and I know them to be mine.
Prisoner. Q. Was not this on the 10th? A. No; it was on the 15th. - I am certain of that.
FRANCIS JAMES GRUBB. I am the son of Sarah Grubb; I came home from school to her lodgings on the 15th of February, a little before one o'clock; the door then was ajar," and the bolt of the lock shot out, and the drawers were open, and four of the bolts of the locks were up; I did not miss any thing.
THOMAS HARRISON. I am an officer; on the 15th of February I was in Whitecross-street, at a quarter-past eleven o'clock in the morning: I saw the prisoner turn out of Red Lion-market into Whitecross-street; he had this bundle under his arm, tied up in this handkerchief - he went about twenty yards, and into a clothes shop. I followed him, and saw the bundle on the counter; I asked what it was in it; he said some clothes, which he had bought in the lane; I told him to untie it, which he did; I saw these three gowns and this shawl in it - I told him to tie them up again; I took him to the watch-house with it - I searched him, and found eleven skelton and latchkeys, and eight tea, and one table-spoon in his trousers pocket, and this one skeleton key in his waistcoat pocket; he said he bought them all in the lane; I took him before the Magistrate, and traced the owner of the property.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 19.
Recommended to Mercy by Prosecutrix on account of his Aged Parents.
[trialAccount t18280410-275]
1066. JAMES JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of February, 42 lbs. of pork, value 20s. the goods of Thomas Taylor.
THOMAS TAYLOR. I am a salesman of Newgate-market. On the 27th of February, when I came to business in the morning I looked among my legs of pork in my shop, which is opposite my dwelling-house in Rose-street, and missed two legs; I had seen it safe the morning before at nine o'clock; I sent for the beadle who brought the two legs of pork to me, and I knew them to be mine from some mark upon them, and they were from a sty hog, not particularly fat, but very large; my clerk had ticketed those two legs of pork, and I saw the tickets on them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is the paper here that was on them? A. No, it had been tucked into the fat of the meat, and was so rumpled, that if we had attempted to dry it, it would have melted almost; but it was a sty hog, which carries the mark with it almost; the man is not here who killed it - they were sent to me to be sold - the paper was put into them by some of my people - I cannot say whose writing it was.
COURT. Q. Could a person acquainted with pork be able to swear to a piece as belonging to a sty hog. A. Yes.
CHARLES HART. I am in the prosecutor's employ. On Tuesday, the 26th of February, after the market was over, I took in the legs of pork which were not sold - the next morning there was a report that a man was taken up for stealing two legs - I looked into our shop, and two of my master's were gone; this was about half-past seven o'clock; an officer afterwards came to my master's shop - I described the legs of pork to him, and about nine o'clock, I saw what I believe were Mr. Taylor's legs of pork, in his possession - I have not the least doubt of them.
Cross-examined. Q. Was your master's name on them? A. No; there was a note on them, and two cuts, which I had made to hang them up by, at the sinew of the legs, and they had a black mark in the thick part of the leg. I have seen this man about the place.
JAMES GIBSON. I am a patrol. About five or tenminutes after twelve o'clock, on the night of the 26th of February, I was going up London-house-yard with the officer of the night, and saw the prisoner tying up two legs of pork in a blue apron; we asked what he had got? he said, "My own property" - we asked where he got them? he again said, "They are my property; you may do as you like" - We took him to the watch-house.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is not that a public street? A. It is called a yard, but there are houses: the prisoner said he was a butcher.
THOMAS BLACKMORE. I am keeper of the watch-house. I was with Gibson and saw the prisoner stooping down in London-house-yard; I asked him where he got the pork; he said "If you go to the Cap and Salutation tap. they will give you particulars;" Gibson went there, returned, and said the publican said he had been there, but had left about ten o'clock; the prisoner then said if I would go to one Smith, who worked with Mr. Fisher in the market, he would give me another answer; I went there, and they were shut up; I then asked the prisoner where he lived; he said "Go to old Price;" I left them under his care; I went there and he said he had not - I went back and told the prisoner.
JURY. Q. Did you enquire the next mornign of Fisher? A. Yes; I went and found Smith, his foreman, he told me he never sold him any such thing.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you tell Smith you were an officer? A. Not till afterwards, but they pretty well knew it; I said "I am an officer, and if you sold them to Jones you had better come up to the Hall."
Prisoner's Defence. I have been a working-man about Newgate-market; I throw myself on your mercy.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 29.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 2 Months.
[trialAccount t18280410-276]
SIXTH DAY. WEDNESDAY, APRIL 16.
Third London Jury - before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1067. JOSEPH HUGHES was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 1 truck, value 4l. the goods of George Durrant and others.
MORRIS ELLIS. I am in the employ of George Durraut, Brown and Co. - they are silk men, and live in Winchester-street. On the 14th of February, about five o'clock, a truck was lost from their door, where it usually stood; at half past six o'clock I went to put it away for the night, and it was gone; the next morning the officer came to the counting-house - I went with him to see the truck, and knew it was my master's.
JOHN GLEESON. On the 14th of February, in the evening, I saw the prisoner in company with another man in Rosemary-lane - they offered me a truck for sale - I did not buy it; they went up the Minories with it; the officer came and asked me if they had offered me the truck - I said, Yes; I believe it to be the same that I saw again the next morning; I am sure the prisoner took an active part in offering it for sale.
JAMES ROBERTS. On the 14th of February I saw the prisoner and two other men an Tower-hill, at a quarter past seven o'clock in the evening - they stood talking together for 5 or 6 minutes, when the prisoner, and one of the other men, went to a dark corner in Tower-hill, and took a truck out; they went up to the last witness, and when they separated from him I went to him, and asked what they were going to do with the truck - he said they had offered it him for sale for 15s., and that the wheels were worth two guineas; I pursued them, and took the other one, who was tried and convicted here last Session; the prisoner got away at that time.
JURY. Q. Did you see the prisoner take the truck? A. I have no doubt he is the man; I went to the witness and said, "You know that young man who offered the truck for sale - he said, "Yes; his name is Joseph Hughes."
Prisoner's Defence. That man stopped me, and asked if I wanted a job - I told him, Yes, and he said if I wheeled the truck down the Minories he would give me 6d; I took it down, and he asked if I knew any body who wanted such a thing - I took it to the witness, who offered to buy it, and told me to take it down the first turning; when the officer came I ran away.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280410-277]
1061. MARY CLIFFORD alias BURKE was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of March, 1 gold ring, value 6s., 1 half-crown, and 2s., the property of Henry Harris.
MARY HARRIS. I am the wife of Henry Harris. We live in Augusta-street, Hampstead-road. On the 22d of March, I met the prisoner coming over Blackfriars' - bridge; she was quite a stranger - she nearly stopped me, by seeming to pick up something under my feet; which appeared to be two seals, a ring, and a chain. She stopped me, and said, I should have half; we walked on till we came to the second street over the bridge - she asked me if I knew where there was a silversmiths, I said, No; we walked on till we met a young man - she asked him if he knew where there was a silversmiths; he said, he worked at one - we walked on, and I asked how far she was going to take me - she then asked him the value of them; he said 30l., but we should not get more than 28l. if we went to sell them; she then told me, if I would go with her into the country, she would give me the half part of it - I said I could not; she then told me to give her my ring, and what money I had, and wait at the end of Fleet-market, and she would come to me in an hour and a half, and bring me 12l. I gave her my ring and 4s. 6d., and waited for her till three or four o'clock, but she did not come - I met her again on the following Monday, near the bridge; she crossed the road when she saw me - I crossed after her, and asked her for my ring and my money; she said she did not know what I meant - but I insisted upon having them, or I would raise the street; she had another young man with her then - she said if I would not make a noise, I should have them; we walked on till we met the young man whom I had seen the first time. I asked her again for my property, and she said if I would hold my tongue, I should have them in a few minutes; and the two young men went for it, but they did not return - I waited twenty minutes or half an hour; then called assistance, and gave her into custody.
THOMAS SNUGGS SHARPE. I am a constable. I received charge of the prisoner on the 24th; she said if Iwould let her go the money and ring would be brought to the watch-house; I said that would not do for me, I must lock her up; a woman came to see her; I inquired what she wanted - she very much wished to see her alone; I said I suspected she was a party to the felony, and I must lock her up; she then handed me this ring, this half-crown, a shilling, and some halfpence, and wanted me to let the prisoner go, but I detained them both. The prisoner gave her name as Burke at the watch-house; I ascertained that the ring had been pawned at a shop in Shoe-lane: I went there, and the pawnbroker described the prisoner's person - he knew her by the name of Clifford; she represented herself as a widow.
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.

The prosecutor did not appear NOT GUILTY.

THOMAS SAUNDERS was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 1 coat, value 20s.; 1 waistcoat, value 5s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 10s.; 1 pistol, value 2s.; 1 watch-guard, value 10d.; 3 keys, value 8d., and 2 rings, value 4d., the goods of William Henry Dawson; and 1 razor-strop, value 1s., the goods of Christian George Matthews.
SECOND COUNT, charging a previous conviction for felony at the Quarter Sessions holden at St. Mary, Newington, Surrey, on the 23d of October, 1826.

[trialAccount t18280410-280]
1071. GEORGE CLEAVER was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 2 crowns, 8 half-crowns, 40 shillings, and 20 sixpences, the monies of William Spratt, his master.
WILLIAM SPRATT. The prisoner was my servant. On the 12th of August, 1826, or thereabouts, he absconded; I then kept the Hole in the Wall public-house, in Fleet-street - this money was given to him one Saturday evening, to get his money in from the customers; he was to account to me on the Sunday morning - I never saw him again till the 3d of March last, when I saw him in Fleet-street, and gave charge of him.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Are you in any business now? A. No; I have retired for awhile; I left the Hole-in-the-wall last February; a short time before this happened I was indicted with the prisoner through a troublesome neighbour; I did not tell the prisoner that he was to bear the charge of the indictment; I paid 12s. for Mr. Thompson, who had bailed him; while I was gone to the Nore the prisoner absconded from his bail; I did not stop one farthing of his money for that indictment; I have not treated this as a matter of account; I did receive different sums of money to the amount of 1l. 9s., but I did it with a view to apprehend the prisoner.
COURT. Q. Did you receive these sums as part of payment? A. Yes; in part of the 4l. of silver.
SARAH SPRATT. I am the wife of William Spratt. I gave the prisoner this money on the 12th of August, 1826, to the best of my recollection; he returned in about an hour, and looked round in the tap-room; at nine o'clock I asked for him to take out his beer, but he was gone.
Cross-examined. Q. Had there not been an indictment against him? A. Yes; he did not abscond on account of that; we went down to the Nore - we heard he had been taken up, and that Mr. Thompson had bailed him.
THOMAS LIGHTFOOT. I am an officer, and took the prisoner into custody.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury on account of his master's improper conduct.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18280410-281]
1072. JAMES BARRETT, SAMUEL ROBINSON and JOHN BROWN, were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Nicholas Waylett, about two o'clock in the night of the 8th of April, at St. Leonard, Eastcheap, with intent to steal, and stealing therein. 1 desk, value 2l., his property.
JAMES NICHOLAS WAYLETT. I live at No. 45, Fish-street, in the parish of St. Leonard, Eastcheap. I am a boot and shoe maker; I sleep in Crooked-lane, about one hundred yards off - I was awoke about a quarter past two o'clock in the morning of the 8th of April, by the watchman, who said my boy wanted me at Fish-street-hill: I have a boy sleeps in my shop there - I went there and unlocked the door: I saw my boy standing inside with a mallet in his hand; he said "Sir, there are thieves in the house;" I said "Impossible, you are frightened, the cats have got in:" he said No - I said to the watchman "Give me your staff, and go and get what spare men you have - they came immediately; I lighted several candles, and requested they would go into the cellar and turn on the gas and search the cellar - they did so: I said "Now search this house;" and in the mean time I was watching the adjoining house, which is empty; they were up stairs, they went into the adjoining house through an internal communication which I have, but I was not with them, and in five or six minutes the prisoners were brought out - I did not see anything taken from them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. There is an internal communication between your house and the other? A. Yes, in the party-wall - I do not know whether it was open or not.
COURT. Q. What did you find done? A. When I went into the parlour I found the window open and broken, and the desk was on the floor, which I had seen some time the day before.
JAMES CONNER. I am in the employ of the prosecutor. I slept in this house on the night of the 7th of April; I did not lock up the house that night, but it was fastened - I saw the desk about a quarter-past nine on the stand at the further end of the showroom; between two and three o'clock that morning I heard some persons coughing in the parlour - I made an alarm and cried Watch! One of the prisoners came towards me with a dark lantern and held it to my face - I cried Watch! and they ran up stairs; the man did not come to my bed-side - there was a glass wainscoat between us; I got out of bed and got a mallet and made an alarm against the door; two men were going by, who asked what was the matter; I said there were thieves in the house - I could not get out - I could not open the door; the men called the watchman - he came and asked what was the matter, and I told him to go to Mr. Waylett, who came in about five minutes; I went to search the house - we found the three prisoners in the privy, in the empty house: I saw the desk on the floor in the parlour - there was nothing found on the prisoner's persons; there is a door between the two houses - I had not seen that on the overnight; the glass of the parlour door inside was broken - that is not the glass wainscoat, it is a door that goes up stairs.
RICHARD KING. I am a watchman. I heard the alarm and went and rang for Mr. Waylett: I went and called the patrol - I came back and saw Mr. Waylett unlocking the door, outside; the patrol told me to go round to Pudding-lane - I did so, and saw a watchman there; I told him to watch - I went to the watch-house, and went down to the house again; I searched - I could find no person in the house, but saw some person had made water against the door-post; we then went through the communicating door into the empty house - we went down into the shop, and down into the cellar; we saw no person there - we went up again but saw no one; we went down again, and into the privy, and there we found the three prisoners; this was about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour after we got in.
JOHN BAYLEY. I am a patrol. I went to assist in searching the house; I saw the prisoners searched, but nothing was found on them - this dark lantern, this centre-bit and four skeleton keys were found in the privy.
JAMES CORK. I am a patrol. On this morning I went in with Mr. Waylett's and saw what they have stated; I was in the front part of the building, and King cried out"Here they are:" I saw the prisoners taken in the privy - I found this knife on the stairs.
JAMES WOLSONCROFT. I am shopman to Mr. Waylett. I fastened up his shop on the night of the 7th of April, I went up stairs, and saw the door which communicates between the two houses, between eight and nine o'clock; it was locked with a padlock in the usual way.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was there any servant maid in the house? A. No; no person but me and Conner - he gets his breakfast when Mr. Waylett goes there in the morning; there is no bed in the house but his - I had not touched the door of communication to padlock it, for a month before perhaps; the door is on the second floor - I did not touch it the night before; a person could not have got in there without breaking the door, but that is not where they did get in - I did not see them get in.
COURT. Q. When were you there again? A. Between eight and nine o'clock next morning: there is a window, on the first staircase, which looks on the sky-light of the empty house, and that window was broken to let in two fingers to undo the button, and when that was open it would make an opening plenty large enough to admit the body of a man; I saw the padlock in the staple next morning.
JURY. Q. Is it a glass door between the two houses? A. No; a wainscoat door.
Cross-examined. Q. For ought you know, not having pushed against the door, you cannot say whether it might not have been ajar? A. It is possible; I was up the staircase the night before, and the lock was in the staple - I am not positive of that; my attention was not particularly directed to it.
WILLIAM WIDGEON BEAUMONT. I am the night officer I went to the house and saw the desk on the floor in the parlour; I waited there to protect the shop and then went to the watch-house with the prisoners - I went to the house again and saw the putty had been chipped round the glass of the door which leads from the show-room, up stairs; and the glass broken as if to admit a hand to get at the lock; I went down to the house again in the morning and saw a window on the staircase standing open; I looked through it and saw the mark of an iron heel, and a chalk mark on the leads which lead over the showroom to the empty house, which is a very short distance; I got out and went through an aperture into the empty house - it appeared to have been a window blocked up with wood; I got through it, and searched over every part of the empty house - I saw the door of communication between the two houses; it is lined with iron - I saw no marks of violence on it; the padlock was hanging in the staple, but not locked - I went into the privy and saw something like a bag on the soil, and we got up a bag containing these articles.
Cross-examined. Q. If a passer by had seen the door of communication as you did, they might have thought it was fast? A. I could not bring my mind to believe it was locked before; I could see nothing to induce me to believe it was locked - the door was open when I saw it, and the lock hanging in the staple; if the door had been ajar and I had not gone against it, or pushed it, and the lock had been in the staple, it might have deceived me.
COURT to WOLSONCROFT. Q. Can you positively swear you saw that door at all that night - did you not take it for granted it was locked - can you swear it at all? A. Yes; and I saw nothing particular about it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you examine the door particularly? A. No; I went within about two yards of it; I did not particlarly give it a thought - I might give a glance at it as I passed I cannot tax my memory.
JURY. Q. Did you see the padlock? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Is there any glass over the shop? A. Yes; the button was turned in the staircase window of Mr. Waylett's house; they had stept over the sky-light over the shop to come to the window - nothing was done to the sky-light - it is but small.
BARRETT'S Defence. We were coming from Greenwich fair and had had a little drink: we came down Fish-street-hill, and saw the door of the empty house open - we went in and laid down; some person shut the door while we were there, and we could not find our way out again.
One Witness gave the prisoner Barrett a good character.
BARRETT - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 19.
ROBINSON - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 22.
BROWN - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 35.
[trialAccount t18280410-282]
1073. JOHN WILLIAMS, THOMAS JONES, and HENRY BELL were indicted for stealing, on the 4th March, 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d, the goods of Peter Vernez, from his person.
PETER VERNEZ. On the 4th of March I was coming through Temple-bar; the officer came to me, and said I had been robbed; I felt and missed my handkerchief from my pocket; he then took me back to pursue the three prisoners; he took them into a shop, and secured them - I had my handkerchief within ten minutes before I lost it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
WILLIAM WHITTINGHAM. I am an officer. I saw the three prisoners in company opposite Catherine-street - they followed the prosecutor, and Williams attempted his pocket several times - just as they got to Temple-bar, I saw Williams take the handkerchief out, and fling it back to Bell, who was with Jones, just behind him - I crossed the street and spoke to the prosecutor; they had then got to the Strand; I followed them, and pushed them into a tobacco-shop - this handkerchief was under Bell's arm in a hard lump; I saw Jones with the other two for ten minutes; Bell and him were close behind Williams when he took the handkerchief; they must both have seen him do it; and when I went back after them, Williams and Jones were together, and Bell in front of them.
WILLIAMS - GUILTY. Aged 16.
JONES - GUILTY. Aged 18.
BELL - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18280410-283]
1074. CHARLOTTE LUMLEY, alias RUMLEY , was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 4 1/2 yards of ribbon, value 1s. 2d., the goods of David Reid.
DAVID REID. I keep a haberdasher's shop in Beech-street, Cripplegate. I saw the prisoner in my shop on the 13th of March: I was serving a customer at the opposite counter, and I heard McNab say to her, "What have you done with that roll in your hand" - she said, "I had none"- he said "You had," and he came round the counter - she came back from the counter, and I heard this roll of ribbon drop; I took it up; it is mine - she was about two feet from the counter, and I think it impossible that it could fall from the counter.
ALEXANDER MCNABB. I am in the employ of Mr. Reid - the prisoner came in, and asked for some ribbon; I showed her a drawer of ribbons, but there were none to please her - I showed her a second, and she took up a roll of ribbon in her hand - at that instant another customer came into the shop, to whom I spoke without turning aside, and while I did that, I saw the prisoner roll the ribbon up in her handkerchief - she laid down a child on the counter while she did it, and then took it up, and held the ribbon in her hand under the child's clothes - I showed her some other ribbons, and then I charged her with having that - she denied it - I went round the counter - she went back, and I heard the ribbon fall.
Q. Did she express any surprise, as if the ribbon fell from the child? A. Yes, and she appealed to two people in the shop, if they had seen it in her hand; they said they saw it fall, but had not seen it in her hand.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, declaring that she had not touched the ribbon, but that her child must have thrown it on the ground.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18280410-284]
1044. MARY HODGES and JANE WELCH were indicted for stealing, on the 11th of April, 26 yards of printed cotton, value 13s., the goods of Thomas Hall.
JAMES REYNOLDS. I am shopman to Mr. Thomas Hall, a linen-draper of Bishopsgate-street, without. Last Friday, I saw the two prisoners at the door, but did not see them do anything; I soon after missed a piece of print from the door - in about an hour and a half I saw them again at the window. and Hodges had a large basket in her hand; I went out and looked at the basket, which was covered with a blue apron; they went down the street, and looked at some ear-rings in a jeweller's window - I went to look in the basket, and Welch came and put her cloak between me and the basket, and began pulling her ear-rings, and asked Hodges if she thought they would match; I told Hodges to come back with me; she said, the basket did not belong to her - I said, she could not object to come back with me to our shop; but I took her to Mr. Johnson's shop, and found this print of Mr. Halls, in the basket, and another piece of print, belong to Mr. Needham, of Shoreditch - I went to fetch an officer, and on returning, I met Welch; I told her, she was the very person I was looking for, and she must go back; she said, she would not - I said, if she did not, I would get an officer; I compelled her to go back, and gave charge of them - this is the print; it has our shop mark on it.
WILLIAM WILTSHIRE. I am an officer and took the prisoners.
The prisoners put in written defences, in which Hodges stated that Welch had left her for about ten minutes, and on returning, put something into her basket; but what it was she could not tell. - Welch, in her defence stated that she had found the print in the street, and put it into Hodge's basket.
JAMES REYNOLDS re-examined. Q. How long had you missed the print? A. About an hour and a half; there was a piece of calico drawn over the prints, which led me to suspect something was wrong.
HODGES - GUILTY. Aged 25.
Hodges received a good character, and was recommended to Mercy.
Confined Two Months.
WELCH - GUILTY. Aged 28.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280410-285]
1074. WILLIAM JOHNSON was indicted for a misdemeanour.
EDWARD SHEAR I am shopman to Mr. James Shear, furnishing ironmonger, of Holborn Hill, On the 1st of March the prisoner delivered this order at our shop for three copper tea-kettles, and I delivered them to him, knowing Mr. Asher Prior was a customer of Mr. Shears.(Order read.)
1 March, 1828.
Sir, Please to let the bearer have one 4-quart, one 3-quart, and one 5-pint oval copper tea-kettles, for A. Peror.
51, Marchmont-street.
ASHER PRIOR. I did not write this order, nor authorise the prisoner to take any order from me to Mr. Shear; he was in my employ eighteen months ago; he could not read nor write.
THOMAS GOLD. On Saturday, the 1st of March, I was coming down Beech-street, and saw the prisoner and another young man - I thought they were going to dosomething; I waited and saw the prisoner leave the other at the corner of Beech-street - and in less than five minutes he came back with these kettles in an apron; the other crossed over to him - they went down King's Head-court; I went to them, and asked them what they had got; the other said he had brought them from his master, in Barbican; I said "It is but a little way, let us go there;" we went on; I said, "Where is it?" - he said he did not know, and I detained them; they had been left somewhere, for they had not them in their possession when I first saw them, and he had not time to go to Holborn.
Prisoner's Defence. The other person gave me the order to take, and he is in Clerkenwell to take his trial.
GUILTY.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18280410-286]
1100. SAMUEL TURNER was indicted for a misdemeanour.
The prosecutor did not appear. NOT GUILTY.
